<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We give a uniform deterministic polynomial-time algorithm for complete factorization over prime fields. For a prime <i>p</i> in binary and a nonzero polynomial $`f\in\mathbf F_p[x]`$ given by its dense coefficient list, the algorithm computes the irreducible factors and their multiplicities using a number of bit operations polynomial in $`(\deg f+1)\log p`$. The proof uses the uniform Hecke zero-free theorem from the companion paper *Primitive roots for every admissible integer base*.

- **claim-factorization** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-factorization)): There is a uniform deterministic algorithm that, given a prime $p$ in binary and a nonzero dense polynomial $f\in\mathbf F_p[x]$ of degree $n$, outputs its leading coefficient and its distinct monic irreducible factors with their multiplicities in $$O\bigl(((n+1)\lceil\log_2p\rceil)^{10^{12}}\bigr)$$ bit operations, with absolute implied constant. A constant polynomial returns an empty factor list. The algorithm uses no randomness, integer-factorization oracle, primitive-root oracle, or GRH assumption.
  - Explanation: Main result: a uniform deterministic algorithm factors any nonzero dense polynomial over a prime field, with leading coefficient and multiplicities, in time polynomial in the degree and the bit length of the prime, with no randomness, oracle or GRH assumption.
- **claim-reduction** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-thm-reduction)): There is a uniform deterministic algorithm with the following guarantee. Its input is a prime $p$ and a nonzero dense polynomial $f\in\mathbf F_p[x]$. If $p>B^{200000}$ and $n\ge2$, its input also includes one auxiliary prime $\ell_q$ satisfying <a href="#eq:auxiliary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:auxiliary">[eq:auxiliary]</a> for each prime $q\le n$. It outputs the complete factorization of $f$, including multiplicities. Let $E=\max(2,\max_q\ell_q)$, with $E=2$ when no auxiliary primes are required. For an absolute constant $C$, the algorithm uses $O((B+E)^C)$ bit operations. It uses neither randomness nor an integer factorization or primitive-root oracle.
  - Explanation: Algebraic core: once one auxiliary prime per prime up to the degree is supplied, factorization runs in time polynomial in the parameter B and in the numerical size of the largest auxiliary prime.
- **lemma-simultaneous-execution** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-ff-simultaneous)): Suppose a deterministic field procedure uses arithmetic, zero tests, and integer control, and its scalar input at component $i$ is obtained by specializing given elements of $A$ at $T=x_i$. Assume the procedure is defined for each specialized input. One can either execute the procedure on all components with a common sequence of decisions, or return a nontrivial proper monic divisor of $F$ in $\mathbf F_p[T]$. The extra cost of each scalar operation or zero test is polynomial in $N$, $[k:\mathbf F_p]$, and $\log p$.
  - Explanation: A field procedure can be run on all unknown roots at once in the quotient algebra; either all components follow the same decisions or a zero test produces a proper factor of F.
- **lemma-unit-root** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-ff-unitroot)): Let $k$ be an explicit finite field of cardinality $Q$ and characteristic $p$. Let $q$ be an odd prime dividing $Q-1$, let $\zeta\in k$ have order $q$, and let $\omega\in k$ generate the full $q$-primary subgroup of $k^*$. Let $H\in k[X]$ be monic and square-free, with $h=\deg H\ge1$, and suppose $p>h^2$. Given a unit $a\in k[X]/H$ that is promised to be a $q$-th power, one can deterministically construct $b$ with $b^q=a$. The bit cost is polynomial in $q$, $h$, and the length of the given field representation. The procedure uses only arithmetic and zero tests over $k$, and so admits simultaneous execution as in Lemma <a href="#ff:simultaneous" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ff:simultaneous">3</a>.
  - Explanation: Given a generator of the full primary subgroup, a promised q-th power in a reduced algebra has a root computed deterministically by digit-wise logarithms, without factoring the modulus.
- **prop-even-split** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-ff-even-split)): Let $p$ be odd, and let $F\in\mathbf F_p[T]$ be monic, square-free, and totally split of even degree $N\ge2$, with $p>N$. Given a generator $u$ of the full $2$-primary subgroup of $\mathbf F_p^*$, one can deterministically find a nontrivial proper monic divisor of $F$ in a number of bit operations polynomial in $N$ and $\log p$.
  - Explanation: A totally split square-free polynomial of even degree is split by orienting pairs of roots with the 2-primary generator and comparing row counts, which cannot all coincide for even degree.
- **lemma-degree-q-field** (construction, [source](#stmt-table-degreeq)): The algebra $U_q$ is a field of degree $q$ over $\mathbb F_p$.
  - Explanation: The invariant subalgebra of the cyclotomic algebra under q-th powers is a field of degree q, obtained by linear algebra and the power-residue condition on the auxiliary prime, with no factoring of the cyclotomic polynomial.
- **prop-table-construction** (construction, [source](#stmt-table-construction)): Suppose the auxiliary primes <a href="#table:primes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="table:primes">[table:primes]</a> are supplied, and suppose a deterministic procedure has the following uniform contract: for every integer $1\le b<n$, it factors every monic polynomial of degree at most $b$ over $\mathbb F_p$ using table entries only for primes at most $b$. Then the complete table through $n$ can be built in increasing prime order. Its construction calls that procedure at most once for each odd prime $q\le n$, on a polynomial of degree $q-1$; each such call uses only already constructed table entries.* *Apart from these factorization calls, the construction has bit cost polynomial in $E$, $n$, and $\log p$, with an absolute exponent. Each stored odd-prime field has degree at most $q-1$, and the temporary field $V_q$ has degree at most $q(q-1)$ over $\mathbb F_p$.
  - Explanation: The table of primary generators is built in increasing prime order, calling factorization only in strictly smaller degree, so no factorization oracle is needed.
- **lemma-torsion-filtration** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geom-filtration)): The module $T_t$ is killed by $\lambda^t$. The map $$T_t\longrightarrow T_{t+1},\qquad (d,W)\longmapsto(d,\lambda W)$$ is injective, and its image is $T_{t+1}[\lambda^t]$. Consequently, inside any $T_m$ the successive modules are precisely $T_j=T_m[\lambda^j]$ for $1\le j\le m$.
  - Explanation: The modules pairing a divisor class with an infinity divisor form a torsion filtration in which each level embeds as the lambda-torsion of the next.
- **lemma-ramification-labels** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geom-ramification)): In the setup of Section <a href="#geom:setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:setup">4.1</a>, the elements $$\epsilon_i=([P_i-\infty_0],e) \quad(1\le i\le N)$$ generate $T_1$ over $\mathbb F_q$, and $$\sum_{i=1}^N\epsilon_i=0. \label{geom:sum-epsilon}$$ More explicitly, let $D$ be a geometric degree-zero divisor, let $W\in\Lambda$, and suppose that $$\operatorname{div}(h)=(1-\sigma)D-W.$$ Then $\operatorname{Norm}_{\overline K(C)/\overline K(X)}(h)$ is a nonzero constant. Choose $\gamma\in\overline K^*$ with $$\gamma^q=\operatorname{Norm}_{\overline K(C)/\overline K(X)}(h).$$ Then $h(P_i)$ is defined and nonzero, and the residues $$l_i=v_{P_i}(D)-\log_\zeta\bigl(h(P_i)/\gamma\bigr) \quad\text{in }\mathbb F_q \label{geom:labels}$$ satisfy $$([D],W)=\sum_{i=1}^N l_i\epsilon_i \quad\text{in }T_1. \label{geom:label-expansion}$$ Here $v_{P_i}(D)$ is the coefficient of $P_i$ in $D$, and $\log_\zeta$ is the unique exponent modulo $q$ of a $q$-th root of unity.
  - Explanation: The first torsion module is generated by the ramification point classes, which sum to zero; labels computed from a function with the stated divisor give the coordinates of any pair in this basis.
- **lemma-frobenius-rational** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geom-frobenius)): For the curve and modules above, let $r\ge0$, set $m=1+(q-1)r$, and let $k/K$ be the extension of degree $q^r$. Then $\Pi^{q^r}$ fixes $T_m$ pointwise. If $1\le t\le m$ and $d\in J$, $W\in\Lambda$ satisfy $\lambda^t d=[W]$, then $d$ itself is fixed by the Frobenius of $k$.
  - Explanation: A power of Frobenius fixes the higher torsion module pointwise, so divisions through the filtration are rational over the extension of the stated degree.
- **lemma-finite-field-descent** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geom-descent)): Let $C$ be a smooth projective geometrically integral curve over a finite field $k$. A $k$-rational divisor that is geometrically principal is the divisor of a function in $k(C)^*$. Every geometric divisor class fixed by the Frobenius of $k$ has a $k$-rational divisor representative.
  - Explanation: Over a finite field, geometrically principal rational divisors are divisors of rational functions, and Frobenius-fixed classes have rational divisor representatives.
- **cor-rational-lifts** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geom-lifts)): In the setup of Section <a href="#geom:setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:setup">4.1</a>, let $r\ge0$, $m=1+(q-1)r$, and $[k:K]=q^r$. For every $i$ there exist classes $d_{i,1},\ldots,d_{i,m}$, all represented by degree-zero divisors over $k$, such that $$\begin{split} d_{i,1}&=[P_i-\infty_0],\\ \lambda d_{i,t+1}&=d_{i,t}\quad(1\le t<m),\\ \lambda^t d_{i,t}&=[e]\quad(1\le t\le m). \end{split} \label{geom:lift-chain}$$ Moreover, every successive choice of a geometric division in such a chain has a representative over $k$.
  - Explanation: Each ramification class admits a chain of successive lambda-divisions all represented by degree-zero divisors over the working field, for every choice of divisions.
- **prop-forced-separation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geom-separation)): In the setup of Section <a href="#geom:setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:setup">4.1</a>, put $r=v_q(N)$ and $m=1+(q-1)r$, and let $k/K$ have degree $q^r$. Let $d_m\in J$ have a degree-zero divisor representative over $k$ and satisfy $\lambda^m d_m=[Ne]$. For $1\le t\le m$, set $$d_t=\lambda^{m-t}d_m,\qquad W_t=\lambda^{-(t-1)}Ne\in\Lambda, \label{geom:separation-data}$$ and choose any degree-zero divisor $D_t$ over $k$ representing $d_t$. The lattice divisions defining $W_t$ exist and are unique.* *Starting at $t=1$, make the following test, advancing to $t+1$ only when all labels at $t$ agree. Choose $h_t\in k(C)^*$ and $\gamma_t\in k^*$ such that $$\operatorname{div}(h_t)=(1-\sigma)D_t-W_t, \qquad \gamma_t^q=\operatorname{Norm}(h_t), \label{geom:separation-functions}$$ and form the labels $$l_{i,t}=v_{P_i}(D_t) -\log_\zeta\bigl(h_t(P_i)/\gamma_t\bigr) \quad\text{in }\mathbb F_q. \label{geom:separation-labels}$$ At every reached test these choices exist over $k$ and the labels are defined. For some reached $t\le m$, the labels are not all equal. This conclusion holds for every choice of the representatives $D_t$ and of the functions and roots in <a href="#geom:separation-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:separation-functions">[geom:separation-functions]</a>.
  - Explanation: Running the label tests along the two chains must produce unequal labels at some reached level, since equal labels throughout would force an impossible extra lambda-division in the infinity lattice.
- **prop-divisor-arithmetic** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-div-arithmetic)): On the curve $C$ above, the following operations are deterministic and use a number of field operations polynomial in $q,N$, the degrees occurring in the input representation, the absolute degrees of the divisors materialized during the operation, and the parameter $\ell$ in the third operation:* 1. *divisor addition, negation, coefficientwise minimum and maximum, and the action of $\sigma$;* 2. *construction of the divisor of an explicitly represented nonzero function, and evaluation of its valuation and leading coefficient at a specified ramification point or point at infinity;* 3. *computation of the Riemann–Roch space $L(D+\ell\infty_0)$ for $\ell\geq 0$;* 4. *replacement of a degree-zero divisor $D$ by a linearly equivalent divisor $D'$ satisfying $\\|D'\\|\leq 2g$, with a function $a$ such that $D'=D+\operatorname{div}(a)$.* *These algorithms do not require factorization of the polynomials defining the finite supports. Their decisions use only field zero tests, so they admit simultaneous execution as in Lemma <a href="#ff:simultaneous" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ff:simultaneous">3</a>.
  - Explanation: Divisor arithmetic, function divisors, Riemann–Roch spaces and reduction of degree-zero divisors are done deterministically in polynomially many field operations, without factoring supports, using only zero tests.
- **lemma-compression** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-div-compression)): From the representation of $I=I(D)$ one can compute monic polynomials $h^+,h^-\in k[X]$ satisfying $$\deg h^+=\sum_{P\text{ finite}}\max(v_P(D),0)[k(P):k],\qquad \deg h^-=\sum_{P\text{ finite}}\max(-v_P(D),0)[k(P):k].$$ The polynomial $H=h^+h^-$ satisfies <a href="#div:bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="div:bound">[div:bound]</a>. If $\deg D=0$ and $j=h^+/h^-$, then $$\label{div:orbitnorm} \operatorname{div}_C(j)=\sum_{a=0}^{q-1}\sigma^aD.$$
  - Explanation: From the ideal representation of a divisor one computes monic polynomials bounding its positive and negative parts, and their quotient has divisor equal to the orbit sum for degree-zero divisors.
- **lemma-divisor-sum** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-div-trace)): Suppose $A\simeq k^s$ is an explicitly represented split algebra. Let $I_1,\ldots,I_s$ be fractional ideals of $O$ supplied together over $A$, with monic bounds $H_i$ and a common number $b$ of generators $u_{i0},\ldots,u_{i,b-1}$, including $H_i$. If the prime subfield contains more than $s(b-1)$ elements, their product can be computed by polynomial arithmetic, determinants, and ideal closure, without a decomposition of $A$. Together with addition of the infinity vectors, this computes the sum of the corresponding divisors.
  - Explanation: A family of divisors given over a split algebra is summed over the base field via norms and determinants, without decomposing the algebra, provided the prime subfield is large enough.
- **lemma-large-infinity-circuits** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-div-circuits)): Let $D$ be a represented degree-zero divisor of bounded absolute degree, and let $W=\sum_jw_j\infty_j$ have degree zero, with integer coefficients given in binary. If $Z=(1-\sigma)D-W$ is principal, a circuit for a function $h$ satisfying $\operatorname{div}(h)=Z$ can be computed using a number of operations polynomial in $q,N,\\|D\\|$, the representation size, and $\max_j\log(2+\|w_j\|)$. The circuit uses multiplication and inversion of explicitly represented small functions. At each known ramification point $P$, it computes the nonzero value $h(P)$ within the same type of bound, without expanding $h$.
  - Explanation: A principal function whose divisor has binary-sized infinity coefficients is stored as a circuit built by doubling and addition, and its value at a ramification point is computed without expanding it.
- **thm-norm-solver** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-norm-solve)): Let $k$ be an explicitly represented finite field of characteristic $p$, let $q$ be an odd prime, and suppose that a primitive $q$th root of unity $\zeta\in k$ and a generator of the full $q$-primary subgroup of $k^*$ are given. Let $F\in k[X]$ be monic and square-free, of positive degree $N$ divisible by $q$. Put $$K_0=k(X),\qquad \mathcal L=K_0[Y]/(Y^q-F),\qquad \rho(Y)=\zeta Y.$$ Let $G\in K_0^*$, write $D$ for the maximum of its reduced numerator and denominator degrees, and assume $$\label{norm:characteristic} p>(N+2D+1)^2.$$ If $G$ belongs to $\operatorname{Norm}_{\mathcal L/K_0}(\mathcal L^*)$, a deterministic algorithm constructs $h\in \mathcal L^*$ with $$\prod_{j=0}^{q-1}\rho^j(h)=G.$$ Its bit complexity, and the degrees of the rational coefficients of $h$ in the basis $1,Y,\ldots,Y^{q-1}$, are bounded by fixed polynomials in $q,N,D$, and $\log\|k\|$. It uses no polynomial factorization oracle. Its field-dependent branches use only zero tests, so the algorithm admits the simultaneous execution of Lemma <a href="#ff:simultaneous" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ff:simultaneous">3</a>.
  - Explanation: A promised norm equation in a cyclic extension of a rational function field is solved deterministically in polynomial time via a rank-one idempotent in the cyclic algebra, using no factorization oracle.
- **lemma-idempotent-to-norm** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-norm-idempotent-to-norm)): Given a rank-one idempotent $e\in\mathcal E$, linear algebra over $K_0$ constructs a norm solution $h\in \mathcal L$.
  - Explanation: A rank-one idempotent in the cyclic algebra yields a norm solution by solving one linear equation over the rational function field.
- **lemma-bundle-splitting** (theorem, [source](#stmt-norm-bundle-split)): Every vector bundle on $\mathbb P^1_k$ is a direct sum of line bundles $\mathcal O(d)$, with integer degrees $d$.
  - Explanation: Grothendieck-type splitting over the given field; it is used only to know the block triangular shape of the fiber algebra, not to compute a splitting.
- **lemma-fiber-idempotent** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-norm-fiber-idempotent)): Given a basis of a subalgebra $\mathcal B\subseteq M_q(k)$ that is conjugate to a full block upper triangular algebra, linear algebra over $k$ constructs a rank-one idempotent in $\mathcal B$.
  - Explanation: From a basis of a subalgebra conjugate to a block upper triangular algebra, a rank-one idempotent is found from the kernel of the trace pairing and a common kernel vector, without finding the conjugating matrix.
- **lemma-local-bounds** (bound, [source](#stmt-norm-local-bounds)): At a place with one of the matrix models above, every nonzero entry of the matrices of $W_{ab}$ and $W_{ab}^{-1}$ has valuation between $-M$ and $M$. At a place with the scaled-basis order, the exponents changing between that basis and $W_{ab}$ have absolute value at most $M$. Consequently, the standard coefficients of every local-order element have valuation at least $-M$ at the exceptional places and at infinity. At all other finite places those coefficients are integral.
  - Explanation: The matrix models of the local orders have entries with valuations bounded by the parameter M, which makes the ansatz for global sections finite.
- **prop-class-division** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-div-divide)): Let $k$, $q$, $F$, $C$, and $\sigma$ be as in Proposition <a href="#div:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="div:arithmetic">14</a>, and suppose the auxiliary primary-group data required by Theorem <a href="#norm:solve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:solve">18</a> are available. Let $P=(x,0)$ be a specified $k$-rational ramification point. Given a represented degree-zero divisor $D$ satisfying $\operatorname{char}k>(N+2\\|D\\|+1)^2$, under the promise that $$[D]\in(1-\sigma)\operatorname{Pic}^0(C)(k),$$ there is a deterministic procedure $\operatorname{LambdaDivide}(D,P)$ returning a degree-zero divisor $D'$ with $$\\|D'\\|\leq2g,\qquad (1-\sigma)[D']=[D].$$ It uses polynomially many field operations in the representation size, $q,N,\\|D\\|$, and $\log\|k\|$, with the uniform norm bound from Theorem <a href="#norm:solve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:solve">18</a>. It is compatible with simultaneous execution over the algebra of the unknown roots of $F$.
  - Explanation: A promised lambda-divisible degree-zero divisor class is divided effectively by solving a norm equation normalized at a ramification point and taking a coefficientwise maximum of partial orbit sums.
- **prop-odd-split** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-odd-split)): Let $p$ be prime, $L=\lceil\log_2p\rceil$, and $B=20+(n+1)(L+1)$. Suppose that $p>B^{200000}$ and that $F\in\mathbb F_p[X]$ is monic, square-free, totally split, and of odd degree $3\le N\le n$. Choose an odd prime $q\mid N$. Suppose that the following data are supplied explicitly:* - *a field $K/\mathbb F_p$ of degree at most $q-1$;* - *a primitive $q$-th root of unity $\zeta\in K$;* - *an element $\omega_K\in K^*$ of order $q^{v_q(\|K\|-1)}$.* *There is a deterministic algorithm, denoted by $\mathrm{OddSplit}$, that returns a nontrivial proper monic divisor of $F$ over $\mathbb F_p$ in a number of bit operations bounded by a fixed polynomial in $B$.
  - Explanation: A totally split square-free polynomial of odd degree is split deterministically by lifting ramification classes, summing the lifts and testing labels, in time polynomial in B.
- **lemma-separating-element** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-driver-separator)): Let $h\in\mathbf F_p[X]$ be monic and square-free of degree $d$, let $r=\dim\mathcal B_h\ge2$, and suppose $p>r^3$. From any ordered basis $b_0,\ldots,b_{r-1}$ of $\mathcal B_h$, one can find an integer $t\in\{0,\ldots,r^3\}$ such that the characteristic polynomial $F_t$ of multiplication by $$b(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}t^j b_j \quad\text{on }\mathcal B_h$$ is square-free. The polynomial $F_t$ is monic, totally split over $\mathbf F_p$, and has degree $r$. Every nontrivial proper monic divisor $S$ of $F_t$ gives a nontrivial proper factor $\gcd(h,S(b(t)))$ of $h$. The construction has bit complexity polynomial in $d$ and $\log p$.
  - Explanation: A small integer parameter search gives an element of the Berlekamp algebra whose characteristic polynomial is square-free and totally split, so factors of it pull back to factors of the input.
- **prop-factor-correct** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-driver-factor)): Fix $n$, $p$, and $B$ as in Equation <a href="#driver:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="driver:parameters">[driver:parameters]</a>. For every monic $h\in\mathbf F_p[X]$ of degree $b\le n$, $\mathsf{Factor}(h)$ terminates and returns its complete irreducible factorization. In the large-characteristic case this conclusion assumes the primary table through $b$; no entry for a prime larger than $b$ is used. The number of recursive calls for a single input is $O((b+1)^2)$.
  - Explanation: The recursive driver handles multiplicities and splitting and returns the complete factorization, using table entries only up to the current degree, with a quadratic number of recursive calls.
- **prop-complexity-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-complexity-bound)): Let $E$ be the largest supplied auxiliary prime, or $2$ when none is needed, as in Theorem <a href="#thm:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:reduction">2</a>. The complete algorithm uses $$O\left(B^{500000}+B^{100}E^{20}\right) \label{complexity:total}$$ bit operations. The same bound applies if the auxiliary primes are found by upward search and $E$ denotes the largest prime found. In particular, if $E=O(B^{20000000})$ with an absolute implied constant, the bit cost is $O(((n+1)L)^{10^{12}})$.
  - Explanation: Total bit cost of the complete algorithm is bounded polynomially in B and in the largest auxiliary prime, giving the stated exponent when that prime is bounded by a fixed power of B.
- **thm-hecke-zero-free** (theorem, [source](#stmt-analytic-hecke)): For every cyclotomic number field $K$ containing the twelfth roots of unity, and every finite-order Hecke character $\chi$ of $K$, the Hecke $L$-function $L_K(s,\chi)$ has no zero in $$\Re s>1-\delta,\qquad \delta=10^{-6}.$$ The principal character is included, with its pole at $s=1$ permitted. There is no restriction on conductor or imaginary part.
  - Explanation: Imported analytic input from the companion paper: a uniform zero-free strip for finite-order Hecke L-functions of cyclotomic fields with the twelfth roots of unity, with no restriction on conductor or height.
- **lemma-smoothed-prime-ideals** (bound, [source](#stmt-analytic-explicit)): If the Dedekind zeta function $\zeta_M(s)$ has no zero in $\Re s>1-\delta$, for the fixed $\delta$ in Theorem <a href="#analytic:hecke" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="analytic:hecke">28</a>, then, uniformly for $x\ge2$, $$\label{eq:smooth-prime} \Psi_M(x)=x\Phi(1)+ O_\varphi\bigl(x^{1-\delta}(\log D_M+d_M)\bigr).$$ The implied constant is independent of $M$.
  - Explanation: Given the zero-free strip for the Dedekind zeta function, the smoothed prime-ideal sum has main term plus an error controlled uniformly by discriminant and degree.
- **prop-auxiliary-prime-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-analytic-auxiliary)): There is an absolute constant $c_0$ such that, for every prime $p>B^{200000}$ and every prime $q\le n$, an auxiliary prime for $(p,q)$ exists with $$\ell\le c_0B^{20000000}.$$
  - Explanation: An auxiliary prime exists below a fixed power of B for every large prime characteristic and every prime up to the degree, obtained by comparing smoothed prime counts in a cyclotomic field and its Kummer extension.

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-ff-unitroot",
        "exact_quote": "Let $k$ be an explicit finite field of cardinality $Q$ and characteristic $p$. Let $q$ be an odd prime dividing $Q-1$, let $\\zeta\\in k$ have order $q$, and let $\\omega\\in k$ generate the full $q$-primary subgroup of $k^*$. Let $H\\in k[X]$ be monic and square-free, with $h=\\deg H\\ge1$, and suppose $p>h^2$. Given a unit $a\\in k[X]/H$ that is promised to be a $q$-th power, one can deterministically construct $b$ with $b^q=a$. The bit cost is polynomial in $q$, $h$, and the length of the given field representation. The procedure uses only arithmetic and zero tests over $k$, and so admits simultaneous execution as in Lemma <a href=\"#ff:simultaneous\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"ff:simultaneous\">3</a>.",
        "source_id": "PM-142.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-table-construction",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose the auxiliary primes <a href=\"#table:primes\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"table:primes\">[table:primes]</a> are supplied, and suppose a deterministic procedure has the following uniform contract: for every integer $1\\le b<n$, it factors every monic polynomial of degree at most $b$ over $\\mathbb F_p$ using table entries only for primes at most $b$. Then the complete table through $n$ can be built in increasing prime order. Its construction calls that procedure at most once for each odd prime $q\\le n$, on a polynomial of degree $q-1$; each such call uses only already constructed table entries.*\n\n*Apart from these factorization calls, the construction has bit cost polynomial in $E$, $n$, and $\\log p$, with an absolute exponent. Each stored odd-prime field has degree at most $q-1$, and the temporary field $V_q$ has degree at most $q(q-1)$ over $\\mathbb F_p$.",
        "source_id": "PM-142.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-table-construction",
          "exact_quote": "Suppose the auxiliary primes <a href=\"#table:primes\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"table:primes\">[table:primes]</a> are supplied, and suppose a deterministic procedure has the following uniform contract: for every integer $1\\le b<n$, it factors every monic polynomial of degree at most $b$ over $\\mathbb F_p$ using table entries only for primes at most $b$. Then the complete table through $n$ can be built in increasing prime order. Its construction calls that procedure at most once for each odd prime $q\\le n$, on a polynomial of degree $q-1$; each such call uses only already constructed table entries.*\n\n*Apart from these factorization calls, the construction has bit cost polynomial in $E$, $n$, and $\\log p$, with an absolute exponent. Each stored odd-prime field has degree at most $q-1$, and the temporary field $V_q$ has degree at most $q(q-1)$ over $\\mathbb F_p$.",
          "source_id": "PM-142.1"
        }

        "anchor": "stmt-geom-filtration",
        "exact_quote": "The module $T_t$ is killed by $\\lambda^t$. Consequently, inside any $T_m$ the successive modules are precisely $T_j=T_m[\\lambda^j]$ for $1\\le j\\le m$.",

Choose $\\gamma\\in\\overline K^*$ with $$\\gamma^q=\\operatorname{Norm}_{\\overline K(C)/\\overline K(X)}(h).$$ Then $h(P_i)$ is defined and nonzero, and the residues $$l_i=v_{P_i}(D)-\\log_\\zeta\\bigl(h(P_i)/\\gamma\\bigr)\n                   \\quad\\text{in }\\mathbb F_q\n\\label{geom:labels}$$ satisfy $$([D],W)=\\sum_{i=1}^N l_i\\epsilon_i\n                         \\quad\\text{in }T_1.\n\\label{geom:label-expansion}$$ Here $v_{P_i}(D)$ is the coefficient of $P_i$ in $D$, and $\\log_\\zeta$ is the unique exponent modulo $q$ of a $q$-th root of unity.",

        "anchor": "stmt-geom-lifts",
        "exact_quote": "In the setup of Section <a href=\"#geom:setup\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"geom:setup\">4.1</a>, let $r\\ge0$, $m=1+(q-1)r$, and $[k:K]=q^r$. For every $i$ there exist classes $d_{i,1},\\ldots,d_{i,m}$, all represented by degree-zero divisors over $k$, such that $$\\begin{split}\n d_{i,1}&=[P_i-\\infty_0],\\\\\n \\lambda d_{i,t+1}&=d_{i,t}\\quad(1\\le t<m),\\\\\n \\lambda^t d_{i,t}&=[e]\\quad(1\\le t\\le m).\n \\end{split}\n\\label{geom:lift-chain}$$ Moreover, every successive choice of a geometric division in such a chain has a representative over $k$.",

      },

For every $i$ there exist classes $d_{i,1},\\ldots,d_{i,m}$, all represented by degree-zero divisors over $k$, such that $$\\begin{split}\n d_{i,1}&=[P_i-\\infty_0],\\\\\n \\lambda d_{i,t+1}&=d_{i,t}\\quad(1\\le t<m),\\\\\n \\lambda^t d_{i,t}&=[e]\\quad(1\\le t\\le m).\n \\end{split}\n\\label{geom:lift-chain}$$ Moreover, every successive choice of a geometric division in such a chain has a representative over $k$.",

      "exact_statement": "In the setup of Section <a href=\"#geom:setup\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"geom:setup\">4.1</a>, let $r\\ge0$, $m=1+(q-1)r$, and $[k:K]=q^r$.       "explanation": "Each ramification class admits a chain of successive lambda-divisions all represented by degree-zero divisors over the working field, for every choice of divisions.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-geom-separation",
        "exact_quote": "In the setup of Section <a href=\"#geom:setup\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"geom:setup\">4.1</a>, put $r=v_q(N)$ and $m=1+(q-1)r$, and let $k/K$ have degree $q^r$. Let $d_m\\in J$ have a degree-zero divisor representative over $k$ and satisfy $\\lambda^m d_m=[Ne]$. For $1\\le t\\le m$, set $$d_t=\\lambda^{m-t}d_m,\\qquad\n           W_t=\\lambda^{-(t-1)}Ne\\in\\Lambda,\n\\label{geom:separation-data}$$ and choose any degree-zero divisor $D_t$ over $k$ representing $d_t$. The lattice divisions defining $W_t$ exist and are unique.*\n\n\n*Starting at $t=1$, make the following test, advancing to $t+1$ only when all labels at $t$ agree. Choose $h_t\\in k(C)^*$ and $\\gamma_t\\in k^*$ such that $$\\operatorname{div}(h_t)=(1-\\sigma)D_t-W_t,\n       \\qquad \\gamma_t^q=\\operatorname{Norm}(h_t),\n\\label{geom:separation-functions}$$ and form the labels $$l_{i,t}=v_{P_i}(D_t)\n               -\\log_\\zeta\\bigl(h_t(P_i)/\\gamma_t\\bigr)\n                    \\quad\\text{in }\\mathbb F_q.\n\\label{geom:separation-labels}$$ At every reached test these choices exist over $k$ and the labels are defined. For some reached $t\\le m$, the labels are not all equal. This conclusion holds for every choice of the representatives $D_t$ and of the functions and roots in <a href=\"#geom:separation-functions\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"geom:separation-functions\">[geom:separation-functions]</a>.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-div-arithmetic",
        "exact_quote": "On the curve $C$ above, the following operations are deterministic and use a number of field operations polynomial in $q,N$, the degrees occurring in the input representation, the absolute degrees of the divisors materialized during the operation, and the parameter $\\ell$ in the third operation:*\n\n1.  *divisor addition, negation, coefficientwise minimum and maximum, and the action of $\\sigma$;*\n\n2.  *construction of the divisor of an explicitly represented nonzero function, and evaluation of its valuation and leading coefficient at a specified ramification point or point at infinity;*\n\n3.  *computation of the Riemann–Roch space $L(D+\\ell\\infty_0)$ for $\\ell\\geq 0$;*\n\n4.  *replacement of a degree-zero divisor $D$ by a linearly equivalent divisor $D'$ satisfying $\\|D'\\|\\leq 2g$, with a function $a$ such that $D'=D+\\operatorname{div}(a)$.*\n\n*These algorithms do not require factorization of the polynomials defining the finite supports. Their decisions use only field zero tests, so they admit simultaneous execution as in Lemma <a href=\"#ff:simultaneous\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"ff:simultaneous\">3</a>.",
        "source_id": "PM-142.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-div-arithmetic",
          "exact_quote": "On the curve $C$ above, the following operations are deterministic and use a number of field operations polynomial in $q,N$, the degrees occurring in the input representation, the absolute degrees of the divisors materialized during the operation, and the parameter $\\ell$ in the third operation:*\n\n1.  *divisor addition, negation, coefficientwise minimum and maximum, and the action of $\\sigma$;*\n\n2.  *construction of the divisor of an explicitly represented nonzero function, and evaluation of its valuation and leading coefficient at a specified ramification point or point at infinity;*\n\n3.  *computation of the Riemann–Roch space $L(D+\\ell\\infty_0)$ for $\\ell\\geq 0$;*\n\n4.  *replacement of a degree-zero divisor $D$ by a linearly equivalent divisor $D'$ satisfying $\\|D'\\|\\leq 2g$, with a function $a$ such that $D'=D+\\operatorname{div}(a)$.*\n\n*These algorithms do not require factorization of the polynomials defining the finite supports.           "source_id": "PM-142.1"

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-div-compression",
        "exact_quote": "From the representation of $I=I(D)$ one can compute monic polynomials $h^+,h^-\\in k[X]$ satisfying $$\\deg h^+=\\sum_{P\\text{ finite}}\\max(v_P(D),0)[k(P):k],\\qquad\n \\deg h^-=\\sum_{P\\text{ finite}}\\max(-v_P(D),0)[k(P):k].$$ The polynomial $H=h^+h^-$ satisfies <a href=\"#div:bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"div:bound\">[div:bound]</a>. If $\\deg D=0$ and $j=h^+/h^-$, then $$\\label{div:orbitnorm}\n \\operatorname{div}_C(j)=\\sum_{a=0}^{q-1}\\sigma^aD.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-142.1"
      },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-div-trace",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose $A\\simeq k^s$ is an explicitly represented split algebra. Let $I_1,\\ldots,I_s$ be fractional ideals of $O$ supplied together over $A$, with monic bounds $H_i$ and a common number $b$ of generators $u_{i0},\\ldots,u_{i,b-1}$, including $H_i$. If the prime subfield contains more than $s(b-1)$ elements, their product can be computed by polynomial arithmetic, determinants, and ideal closure, without a decomposition of $A$. Together with addition of the infinity vectors, this computes the sum of the corresponding divisors.",
        "source_id": "PM-142.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-div-circuits",
        "exact_quote": "Let $D$ be a represented degree-zero divisor of bounded absolute degree, and let $W=\\sum_jw_j\\infty_j$ have degree zero, with integer coefficients given in binary. If $Z=(1-\\sigma)D-W$ is principal, a circuit for a function $h$ satisfying $\\operatorname{div}(h)=Z$ can be computed using a number of operations polynomial in $q,N,\\|D\\|$, the representation size, and $\\max_j\\log(2+|w_j|)$. The circuit uses multiplication and inversion of explicitly represented small functions. At each known ramification point $P$, it computes the nonzero value $h(P)$ within the same type of bound, without expanding $h$.",
        "source_id": "PM-142.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "stmt-div-circuits",

          "source_id": "PM-142.1"

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-norm-solve",
        "exact_quote": "Let $k$ be an explicitly represented finite field of characteristic $p$, let $q$ be an odd prime, and suppose that a primitive $q$th root of unity $\\zeta\\in k$ and a generator of the full $q$-primary subgroup of $k^*$ are given. Let $F\\in k[X]$ be monic and square-free, of positive degree $N$ divisible by $q$. Put $$K_0=k(X),\\qquad\n \\mathcal L=K_0[Y]/(Y^q-F),\\qquad \\rho(Y)=\\zeta Y.$$ Let $G\\in K_0^*$, write $D$ for the maximum of its reduced numerator and denominator degrees, and assume $$\\label{norm:characteristic}\n p>(N+2D+1)^2.$$ If $G$ belongs to $\\operatorname{Norm}_{\\mathcal L/K_0}(\\mathcal L^*)$, a deterministic algorithm constructs $h\\in \\mathcal L^*$ with $$\\prod_{j=0}^{q-1}\\rho^j(h)=G.$$ Its bit complexity, and the degrees of the rational coefficients of $h$ in the basis $1,Y,\\ldots,Y^{q-1}$, are bounded by fixed polynomials in $q,N,D$, and $\\log|k|$. It uses no polynomial factorization oracle. Its field-dependent branches use only zero tests, so the algorithm admits the simultaneous execution of Lemma <a href=\"#ff:simultaneous\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"ff:simultaneous\">3</a>.",
        "source_id": "PM-142.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-norm-solve",
          "exact_quote": "Let $k$ be an explicitly represented finite field of characteristic $p$, let $q$ be an odd prime, and suppose that a primitive $q$th root of unity $\\zeta\\in k$ and a generator of the full $q$-primary subgroup of $k^*$ are given. Let $F\\in k[X]$ be monic and square-free, of positive degree $N$ divisible by $q$. Put $$K_0=k(X),\\qquad\n \\mathcal L=K_0[Y]/(Y^q-F),\\qquad \\rho(Y)=\\zeta Y.$$ Let $G\\in K_0^*$, write $D$ for the maximum of its reduced numerator and denominator degrees, and assume $$\\label{norm:characteristic}\n p>(N+2D+1)^2.$$ If $G$ belongs to $\\operatorname{Norm}_{\\mathcal L/K_0}(\\mathcal L^*)$, a deterministic algorithm constructs $h\\in \\mathcal L^*$ with $$\\prod_{j=0}^{q-1}\\rho^j(h)=G.$$ Its bit complexity, and the degrees of the rational coefficients of $h$ in the basis $1,Y,\\ldots,Y^{q-1}$, are bounded by fixed polynomials in $q,N,D$, and $\\log|k|$. It uses no polynomial factorization oracle. Its field-dependent branches use only zero tests, so the algorithm admits the simultaneous execution of Lemma <a href=\"#ff:simultaneous\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"ff:simultaneous\">3</a>.",
          "source_id": "PM-142.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $k$ be an explicitly represented finite field of characteristic $p$, let $q$ be an odd prime, and suppose that a primitive $q$th root of unity $\\zeta\\in k$ and a generator of the full $q$-primary subgroup of $k^*$ are given. Let $F\\in k[X]$ be monic and square-free, of positive degree $N$ divisible by $q$. Put $$K_0=k(X),\\qquad\n \\mathcal L=K_0[Y]/(Y^q-F),\\qquad \\rho(Y)=\\zeta Y.$$ Let $G\\in K_0^*$, write $D$ for the maximum of its reduced numerator and denominator degrees, and assume $$\\label{norm:characteristic}\n p>(N+2D+1)^2.$$ If $G$ belongs to $\\operatorname{Norm}_{\\mathcal L/K_0}(\\mathcal L^*)$, a deterministic algorithm constructs $h\\in \\mathcal L^*$ with $$\\prod_{j=0}^{q-1}\\rho^j(h)=G.$$ Its bit complexity, and the degrees of the rational coefficients of $h$ in the basis $1,Y,\\ldots,Y^{q-1}$, are bounded by fixed polynomials in $q,N,D$, and $\\log|k|$. It uses no polynomial factorization oracle. Its field-dependent branches use only zero tests, so the algorithm admits the simultaneous execution of Lemma <a href=\"#ff:simultaneous\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"ff:simultaneous\">3</a>.",
      "explanation": "A promised norm equation in a cyclic extension of a rational function field is solved deterministically in polynomial time via a rank-one idempotent in the cyclic algebra, using no factorization oracle.",

        "anchor": "stmt-norm-local-bounds",
        "exact_quote": "At a place with one of the matrix models above, every nonzero entry of the matrices of $W_{ab}$ and $W_{ab}^{-1}$ has valuation between $-M$ and $M$. At a place with the scaled-basis order, the exponents changing between that basis and $W_{ab}$ have absolute value at most $M$. Consequently, the standard coefficients of every local-order element have valuation at least $-M$ at the exceptional places and at infinity. At all other finite places those coefficients are integral.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-div-divide",
        "exact_quote": "Let $k$, $q$, $F$, $C$, and $\\sigma$ be as in Proposition <a href=\"#div:arithmetic\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"div:arithmetic\">14</a>, and suppose the auxiliary primary-group data required by Theorem <a href=\"#norm:solve\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"norm:solve\">18</a> are available. Let $P=(x,0)$ be a specified $k$-rational ramification point. Given a represented degree-zero divisor $D$ satisfying $\\operatorname{char}k>(N+2\\|D\\|+1)^2$, under the promise that $$[D]\\in(1-\\sigma)\\operatorname{Pic}^0(C)(k),$$ there is a deterministic procedure $\\operatorname{LambdaDivide}(D,P)$ returning a degree-zero divisor $D'$ with $$\\|D'\\|\\leq2g,\\qquad (1-\\sigma)[D']=[D].$$ It uses polynomially many field operations in the representation size, $q,N,\\|D\\|$, and $\\log|k|$, with the uniform norm bound from Theorem <a href=\"#norm:solve\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"norm:solve\">18</a>. It is compatible with simultaneous execution over the algebra of the unknown roots of $F$.",
        "source_id": "PM-142.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "stmt-div-divide",
          "exact_quote": "Let $k$, $q$, $F$, $C$, and $\\sigma$ be as in Proposition <a href=\"#div:arithmetic\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"div:arithmetic\">14</a>, and suppose the auxiliary primary-group data required by Theorem <a href=\"#norm:solve\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"norm:solve\">18</a> are available. Let $P=(x,0)$ be a specified $k$-rational ramification point. Given a represented degree-zero divisor $D$ satisfying $\\operatorname{char}k>(N+2\\|D\\|+1)^2$, under the promise that $$[D]\\in(1-\\sigma)\\operatorname{Pic}^0(C)(k),$$ there is a deterministic procedure $\\operatorname{LambdaDivide}(D,P)$ returning a degree-zero divisor $D'$ with $$\\|D'\\|\\leq2g,\\qquad (1-\\sigma)[D']=[D].$$ It uses polynomially many field operations in the representation size, $q,N,\\|D\\|$, and $\\log|k|$, with the uniform norm bound from Theorem <a href=\"#norm:solve\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"norm:solve\">18</a>.           "source_id": "PM-142.1"

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-odd-split",
        "exact_quote": "Let $p$ be prime, $L=\\lceil\\log_2p\\rceil$, and $B=20+(n+1)(L+1)$. Suppose that $p>B^{200000}$ and that $F\\in\\mathbb F_p[X]$ is monic, square-free, totally split, and of odd degree $3\\le N\\le n$. Choose an odd prime $q\\mid N$. Suppose that the following data are supplied explicitly:*\n\n- *a field $K/\\mathbb F_p$ of degree at most $q-1$;*\n\n- *a primitive $q$-th root of unity $\\zeta\\in K$;*\n\n- *an element $\\omega_K\\in K^*$ of order $q^{v_q(|K|-1)}$.*\n\n*There is a deterministic algorithm, denoted by $\\mathrm{OddSplit}$, that returns a nontrivial proper monic divisor of $F$ over $\\mathbb F_p$ in a number of bit operations bounded by a fixed polynomial in $B$.",
        "source_id": "PM-142.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-driver-separator",
        "exact_quote": "Let $h\\in\\mathbf F_p[X]$ be monic and square-free of degree $d$, let $r=\\dim\\mathcal B_h\\ge2$, and suppose $p>r^3$. From any ordered basis $b_0,\\ldots,b_{r-1}$ of $\\mathcal B_h$, one can find an integer $t\\in\\{0,\\ldots,r^3\\}$ such that the characteristic polynomial $F_t$ of multiplication by $$b(t)=\\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}t^j b_j\n \\quad\\text{on }\\mathcal B_h$$ is square-free. The polynomial $F_t$ is monic, totally split over $\\mathbf F_p$, and has degree $r$. Every nontrivial proper monic divisor $S$ of $F_t$ gives a nontrivial proper factor $\\gcd(h,S(b(t)))$ of $h$. The construction has bit complexity polynomial in $d$ and $\\log p$.",
        "source_id": "PM-142.1"
      },

        "anchor": "stmt-complexity-bound",
        "exact_quote": "Let $E$ be the largest supplied auxiliary prime, or $2$ when none is needed, as in Theorem <a href=\"#thm:reduction\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:reduction\">2</a>.         "exact_quote": "For every cyclotomic number field $K$ containing the twelfth roots of unity, and every finite-order Hecke character $\\chi$ of $K$, the Hecke $L$-function $L_K(s,\\chi)$ has no zero in $$\\Re s>1-\\delta,\\qquad \\delta=10^{-6}.$$ The principal character is included, with its pole at $s=1$ permitted.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-analytic-explicit",
        "exact_quote": "If the Dedekind zeta function $\\zeta_M(s)$ has no zero in $\\Re s>1-\\delta$, for the fixed $\\delta$ in Theorem <a href=\"#analytic:hecke\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"analytic:hecke\">28</a>, then, uniformly for $x\\ge2$, $$\\label{eq:smooth-prime}\n \\Psi_M(x)=x\\Phi(1)+\n O_\\varphi\\bigl(x^{1-\\delta}(\\log D_M+d_M)\\bigr).$$ The implied constant is independent of $M$.",
        "source_id": "PM-142.1"
      },

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **auxiliary prime for (p,q)** ([source](#eq:auxiliary)): For a prime $q\le n$, call a rational prime $\ell$ an *auxiliary prime for $(p,q)$* if $$\label{eq:auxiliary} \ell\notin\{2,3,p,q\},\qquad \ell\equiv1\pmod{12q},\qquad p^{(\ell-1)/q}\not\equiv1\pmod\ell.$$
- **full q-primary subgroup** ([source](#ff:section)): If $k$ has cardinality $Q$ and $q\mid Q-1$, its full $q$-primary subgroup is the unique subgroup of $k^*$ of order $q^{v_q(Q-1)}$.
- **absolute degree of a divisor** ([source](#sec:divisors)): For a divisor $D$, write $$\\|D\\|=\sum_P \|v_P(D)\|[k(P):k]$$ for its absolute degree.
- **Riemann–Roch space L(V)** ([source](#div:rransatz)): Recall that $$L(V)=\{a\in\mathcal L:\operatorname{div}(a)+V\geq0\}\cup\{0\}$$ is a finite-dimensional $k$-vector space.
- **lattice (local)** ([source](#pm-h-26)): A *lattice* in a finite-dimensional $K_v$-space is a free $R_v$-module of full rank.
- **order and maximal order** ([source](#pm-h-26)): An *order* in $\mathcal E\otimes K_v$ is a lattice that contains $1$ and is closed under multiplication; it is *maximal* if no strictly larger order contains it.
- **smoothed prime-ideal sum** ([source](#pm-h-46)): For a number field $M$ of degree $d_M$ and absolute discriminant $D_M$, define $$\Psi_M(x)=\sum_{\mathfrak p}\sum_{j\ge1} \log\mathop{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak p\, \varphi\bigl((\mathop{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak p)^j/x\bigr),$$ where $\mathfrak p$ runs over nonzero prime ideals of its ring of integers.

| q-norm-characteristic | characteristic needed by the norm solver | > | $(N+2D+1)^2$ | N degree of F, D degree bound of G. |

| q-separator-range | search range for the separating parameter | ≤ | $r^3$ | r the dimension of the algebra. |
| q-recursive-calls | number of recursive calls of the driver | = | $O((b+1)^2)$ | Degree b of the input. |
| q-table-cost | table construction cost outside factorization calls | = | $O\bigl(n(E+1)^{10}(n+\lceil\log_2p\rceil+1)^{40}\bigr)$ | E largest auxiliary prime. |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-hecke-input** [claim](#stmt-analytic-hecke) / [proof](#sec:analytic): Imported theorem from the companion paper; no proof here. -> uniform zero-free strip for finite-order Hecke L-functions
- **step-simultaneous** [claim](#stmt-ff-simultaneous) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ff-simultaneous): Zero tests by gcd in the quotient algebra; a proper gcd gives a factor. -> common execution or proper factor
- **step-unitroot** [claim](#stmt-ff-unitroot) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ff-unitroot): Distinct-degree separation, primary generator by eigenvector search, digit logarithms, Chinese remaindering. -> q-th root in the reduced algebra
- **step-even-split** [claim](#stmt-ff-even-split) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ff-even-split): Orient pairs of roots by the 2-primary logarithm and compare row counts via trace. -> proper factor
- **step-degree-q-field** [claim](#stmt-table-degreeq) / [proof](#proof-stmt-table-degreeq): Frobenius orbits on geometric points of the invariant algebra. -> field of degree q
- **step-table** [claim](#stmt-table-construction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-table-construction): Induction on increasing primes with factorization only of lower degree. -> table of fields and primary generators
- **step-filtration** [claim](#stmt-geom-filtration) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geom-filtration): Direct check using injectivity of lambda on the lattice. -> exact lambda-torsion tower
- **step-ramification** [claim](#stmt-geom-ramification) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geom-ramification): Hilbert 90 for the cyclic cover, invariant divisors, evaluation of labels at ramification points. -> generators and label expansion of the first torsion module
- **step-frobenius** [claim](#stmt-geom-frobenius) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geom-frobenius): Frobenius lowers the filtration; binomial expansion iterated. -> Frobenius power fixes the torsion module
- **step-descent** [claim](#stmt-geom-descent) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geom-descent): Hilbert 90 for constant field extensions and Galois-orbit descent. -> rational divisor or function
- **step-lifts** [claim](#stmt-geom-lifts) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geom-lifts): Surjectivity of lambda on the Jacobian plus Frobenius fixing and descent. -> rational chains of lambda-divisions
- **step-separation** [claim](#stmt-geom-separation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geom-separation): Lattice obstruction: equal labels at every test would force divisibility of an infinity divisor beyond its valuation. -> unequal labels at some test
- **step-compression** [claim](#stmt-div-compression) / [proof](#proof-stmt-div-compression): Characteristic polynomials of multiplication by X on integral ideal quotients. -> bounding polynomials and orbit-sum function
- **step-divisor-arithmetic** [claim](#stmt-div-arithmetic) / [proof](#pm-h-21): Fractional ideals as subspaces, local expansions, Riemann–Roch by linear algebra. -> deterministic divisor operations
- **step-divisor-sum** [claim](#stmt-div-trace) / [proof](#proof-stmt-div-trace): Norms from the split algebra and a grid of scalars. -> sum of divisors over the base field
- **step-circuits** [claim](#stmt-div-circuits) / [proof](#proof-stmt-div-circuits): Binary doubling with reduction and a function circuit tracked by valuations and leading coefficients. -> circuit for the principal function and its values
- **step-idempotent-to-norm** [claim](#stmt-norm-idempotent-to-norm) / [proof](#proof-stmt-norm-idempotent-to-norm): Left ideal of the idempotent identified with the field; solve a linear equation. -> norm solution
- **step-bundle-splitting** [claim](#stmt-norm-bundle-split) / [proof](#proof-stmt-norm-bundle-split): Maximal line subbundle and vanishing of extension groups. -> direct sum of line bundles
- **step-fiber-idempotent** [claim](#stmt-norm-fiber-idempotent) / [proof](#proof-stmt-norm-fiber-idempotent): Kernel of trace pairing and common column kernel. -> rank-one idempotent
- **step-local-bounds** [claim](#stmt-norm-local-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-norm-local-bounds): Explicit matrix models and valuation estimates. -> valuation bounds for the ansatz
- **step-norm-solver** [claim](#stmt-norm-solve) / [proof](#pm-h-34): Five-step linear-algebra algorithm: local models, sections, fiber idempotent, lift, solve. -> norm solution with polynomial bounds
- **step-class-division** [claim](#stmt-div-divide) / [proof](#proof-stmt-div-divide): Normalize the orbit-sum function, solve the norm equation, take the maximum of partial orbit sums. -> divisor representing the division
- **step-odd-split** [claim](#stmt-odd-split) / [proof](#proof-stmt-odd-split): Lift ramification classes, sum, apply powers of lambda, test labels. -> proper factor of an odd-degree split polynomial
- **step-separator** [claim](#stmt-driver-separator) / [proof](#proof-stmt-driver-separator): Counting coincidences of coordinates against the parameter range. -> separating element
- **step-driver** [claim](#stmt-driver-factor) / [proof](#proof-stmt-driver-factor): Induction on degree over the recursive procedure. -> complete factorization
- **step-complexity** [claim](#stmt-complexity-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-complexity-bound): Size accounting for norm, divisor, table and driver costs. -> total bit bound
- **step-explicit-formula** [claim](#stmt-analytic-explicit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-analytic-explicit): Mellin inversion, contour shift, zero counting. -> smoothed prime-ideal estimate
- **step-auxiliary-prime-bound** [claim](#stmt-analytic-auxiliary) / [proof](#proof-stmt-analytic-auxiliary): Compare smoothed counts in a cyclotomic field and its Kummer extension. -> auxiliary prime below a power of B
- **step-reduction** [claim](#stmt-thm-reduction) / [proof](#pm-h-5): Assemble Berlekamp reduction, splitters and table. -> factorization given auxiliary primes
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-factorization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-analytic-auxiliary-2): Search for auxiliary primes upward and apply the reduction. -> unconditional polynomial-time factorization

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-digit-recovery**: For a in the cyclic group generated by u, the base-q digit recovery returns the unique logarithm of a to the base u in the range [0, q^s), one digit per step, using only equality tests against powers of the element u^(q^(s-1)).

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-enumeration-substitute**: Replace the digit procedure by a scan over all group elements to find the logarithm. -> The scan costs time proportional to the group order, which is exponential in the size of the exponent s, while the digit procedure uses a number of equality tests proportional to q per digit.
- **anti-wrong-order**: Run the digit recovery with an element u whose order is not exactly q^s. -> The digit equality has no solution or yields a wrong logarithm, because the argument is not inside the cyclic group the procedure assumes.
- **anti-polynomial-time-claim**: Cite this routine as evidence that the whole factorization runs in time polynomial in the prime p. -> A bound polynomial in p is not polynomial in the binary representation, so the digit routine alone proves no uniform bound for the whole algorithm.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `algorithmic`. The paper states an explicit finite digit-by-digit procedure for logarithms in a cyclic group of prime-power order; this finite step is implemented and cross-checked. The full factorization algorithm is not reproduced.

```python
# INPUTS: one line with five integers p q s u a (prime p, prime q, s>=1, u of exact order q^s mod p, a in the group generated by u)
# PRECONDITIONS: p prime, q prime, u has order exactly q^s in the multiplicative group mod p, a is a power of u
# OUTPUTS: one integer l in [0, q^s) with u^l = a mod p, printed on stdout
# TIME: s digits, each with at most q modular exponentiations
# SPACE: constant number of integers
# ARITHMETIC: exact integer modular arithmetic
# FAILURE: exit code 3 when no digit matches (promise violated)
import sys

def main():
    p, q, s, u, a = map(int, sys.stdin.read().split())
    g = pow(u, q**(s-1), p)
    uinv = pow(u, -1, p)
    l = 0
    for j in range(s):
        t = pow(a * pow(uinv, l, p) % p, q**(s-j-1), p)
        for c in range(q):
            if pow(g, c, p) == t:
                break
        else:
            sys.exit(3)
        l += c*q**j
    print(l)

main()
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper gives a proof-level reduction with explicit but galactic exponents, so there is no runtime artefact to ship as software. The only practical reading is a reference specification of a no-randomness, no-oracle interface. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the statements concerns simulation, real-time state updates or game mechanics; the setting is exact arithmetic over a prime field. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No checker, solver or utility is delivered. The sub-procedures (root extraction, norm solving, divisor reduction) are proof components with impractical bounds, not usable tools. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | stated | The paper states a recursive deterministic factorization driver over prime fields, with explicit steps: handle zero derivative, split off the gcd with the derivative, compute the Berlekamp algebra, find a separating element, split a totally split polynomial, recurse. Constants make it adaptable only as a blueprint, not as a fast implementation. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Divisors are stored as fractional-ideal subspaces and function circuits, but these are working representations inside a proof of polynomial bounds, not a data structure with an operation interface and amortized guarantees. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No parallel, pipelined or circuit-level structure is analysed; only sequential bit-operation counts are given. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A Pohlig–Hellman style digit extraction is used only as an internal logarithm step in primary subgroups. No protocol, hardness assumption or security claim is made. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no learning, statistical estimation or data-driven component; every step is exact algebra over a finite field. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No objective function, constraint set or scheduling problem appears; the linear systems solved are for exact existence and construction, not optimisation. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The procedures are sequential. The word simultaneous refers to computing in a product algebra at all roots at once, not to concurrent or networked processes. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The remaining value is theoretical: derandomized factorization and an analytic-to-algebraic reduction. This is covered by the algorithms entry and is not a separate engineering domain. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-large-characteristic-branch** (hypothesis): The reduction with auxiliary primes is stated only for the large-characteristic branch with degree at least two. Below that threshold the paper uses a different scalar-search driver whose cost grows with the characteristic itself. Blocked use: ['Applying the auxiliary-prime table and curve construction to a characteristic below the stated power of B', 'Quoting the polynomial bound in B and E for inputs in the small-characteristic branch']
- **limit-auxiliary-prime-supply** (hypothesis): The algebraic reduction needs one auxiliary prime for each prime up to the degree. Its cost depends on the numerical size of these primes, so the polynomial bound in B alone holds only when they are bounded by a fixed power of B. Blocked use: ['Treating the reduction as polynomial in B without controlling the largest auxiliary prime', 'Supplying primes that fail the congruence or power-residue condition']
- **limit-companion-analytic-input** (formal_scope): The unconditional size bound for auxiliary primes rests on a zero-free theorem imported from the companion paper and not proved here. The statement is restricted to cyclotomic fields containing the twelfth roots of unity, and the principal character is allowed its pole. Blocked use: ['Presenting the unconditional claim as self-contained in this paper', 'Applying the zero-free strip to number fields that do not contain the twelfth roots of unity']
- **limit-galactic-exponents** (approximation): The exponents are deliberately generous and are not tuned. The stated bounds prove a polynomial-time existence result and say nothing about practical running time. Blocked use: ['Using the bound to predict real running times', 'Presenting the procedure as a replacement for randomized factorizers in practice']
- **limit-even-split-hypotheses** (hypothesis): The even-degree splitter needs odd characteristic larger than the degree and a supplied generator of the full 2-primary subgroup. It applies to square-free, totally split polynomials of even degree. Blocked use: ['Running it in characteristic two', 'Applying it to polynomials of odd degree or not totally split']
- **limit-separator-characteristic** (hypothesis): The separating-element search over the Berlekamp algebra works only when the characteristic exceeds the cube of the algebra dimension, so that the tried parameters are distinct field elements. Blocked use: ['Using the integer parameter search when the characteristic is not larger than the cube of the dimension']
- **limit-norm-solver-characteristic** (hypothesis): The norm solver and the class-division procedure need the characteristic to exceed a quadratic expression in the degree parameters, and the norm equation must be solvable by promise. If the promise fails the construction has no guarantee. Blocked use: ['Calling the norm solver on a function that is not a norm', 'Using it in characteristics below the stated quadratic bound']
- **limit-odd-split-regime** (hypothesis): The odd-degree splitter needs large characteristic, a square-free totally split polynomial of odd degree, an odd prime dividing the degree, and the supplied field, root of unity and primary generator. Blocked use: ['Applying it to even degree', 'Applying it without the primary-group data for the chosen prime']
- **limit-nonconstructive-bundle-shape** (nonconstructive): The block triangular shape of the fiber algebra comes from the splitting of vector bundles on the projective line. It is an existence statement, and no algorithm for computing that splitting is given. Blocked use: ['Reading the bundle splitting as a procedure that outputs the decomposition', 'Deriving a running time for computing the splitting']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We give a uniform deterministic polynomial-time algorithm for complete factorization over prime fields. For a prime <i>p</i> in binary and a nonzero polynomial $`f\in\mathbf F_p[x]`$ given by its dense coefficient list, the algorithm computes the irreducible factors and their multiplicities using a number of bit operations polynomial in $`(\deg f+1)\log p`$. The proof uses the uniform Hecke zero-free theorem from the companion paper *Primitive roots for every admissible integer base*.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Let $p$ be a prime, given in binary, and let $f\in\mathbf F_p[x]$ be a nonzero polynomial, given by its dense coefficient list. Write $n=\deg f$ and $L=\lceil\log_2 p\rceil$. Complete factorization means computing the leading coefficient $c$ and distinct monic irreducible polynomials $g_i$, with positive integer multiplicities $e_i$, such that $$f=c\prod_i g_i^{e_i}.$$ The empty product is permitted when $n=0$. The complexity parameter is $(n+1)L$, so an algorithm polynomial in $p$ does not give a polynomial-time algorithm in the prescribed representation.

Finite-field factorization is a basic constructive problem in algebra. It also exposes a persistent distinction between randomized and deterministic computation: a random element of a product of finite fields often separates its components, whereas constructing a separator uniformly can require additional arithmetic information. The present paper isolates one form of that information and gives a deterministic splitting procedure once it is available. To supply it uniformly, we use the zero-free theorem for finite-order Hecke $L$-functions in the companion paper <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[OpenAIPrimitive26](#ref-OpenAIPrimitive26), Theorem 1.2]. We state that input precisely in Theorem <a href="#analytic:hecke" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="analytic:hecke">28</a> and prove its auxiliary-prime consequence here. The analytic theorem itself belongs to the companion.

<a id="stmt-thm-factorization"></a>
<div id="thm:factorization" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is a uniform deterministic algorithm that, given a prime $p$ in binary and a nonzero dense polynomial $f\in\mathbf F_p[x]$ of degree $n$, outputs its leading coefficient and its distinct monic irreducible factors with their multiplicities in $$O\bigl(((n+1)\lceil\log_2p\rceil)^{10^{12}}\bigr)$$ bit operations, with absolute implied constant. A constant polynomial returns an empty factor list. The algorithm uses no randomness, integer-factorization oracle, primitive-root oracle, or GRH assumption.*

</div>

The exponent is deliberately generous. The point is one fixed polynomial bound for all degrees and prime characteristics. We separate the algebraic construction from its analytic input in the following reduction.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The auxiliary-prime reduction

<a id="eq:B"></a>
<a id="eq:auxiliary"></a>
Put $$\label{eq:B}
 B=20+(n+1)(L+1).$$ For a prime $q\le n$, call a rational prime $\ell$ an *auxiliary prime for $(p,q)$* if $$\label{eq:auxiliary}
 \ell\notin\{2,3,p,q\},\qquad \ell\equiv1\pmod{12q},\qquad
 p^{(\ell-1)/q}\not\equiv1\pmod\ell.$$ Thus $p$ is not a $q$th power modulo $\ell$. The congruence modulo $12q$ is convenient for the analytic construction in Section <a href="#sec:analytic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:analytic">11</a>; the algebraic construction only requires the corresponding congruence modulo $q$.

<a id="stmt-thm-reduction"></a>
<div id="thm:reduction" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2**. *There is a uniform deterministic algorithm with the following guarantee. Its input is a prime $p$ and a nonzero dense polynomial $f\in\mathbf F_p[x]$. If $p>B^{200000}$ and $n\ge2$, its input also includes one auxiliary prime $\ell_q$ satisfying <a href="#eq:auxiliary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:auxiliary">[eq:auxiliary]</a> for each prime $q\le n$. It outputs the complete factorization of $f$, including multiplicities. Let $E=\max(2,\max_q\ell_q)$, with $E=2$ when no auxiliary primes are required. For an absolute constant $C$, the algorithm uses $O((B+E)^C)$ bit operations. It uses neither randomness nor an integer factorization or primitive-root oracle.*

</div>

The dependence on $E$ is on the numerical value of the auxiliary primes, not merely their bit lengths. Consequently Theorem <a href="#thm:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:reduction">2</a> becomes a polynomial-time factorization theorem when these primes are bounded by a fixed power of $B$. Upward search, trial division, and modular exponentiation then construct them within the same kind of bound. We prove this consequence from the cited zero-free theorem in Section <a href="#sec:analytic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:analytic">11</a>. The reduction in Theorem <a href="#thm:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:reduction">2</a> does not use that theorem.

<a id="sec:history"></a>
#### History and the role of the construction

Berlekamp’s algorithms put linear algebra at the center of finite-field factorization: Frobenius-fixed elements in the quotient algebra encode its irreducible factors, and polynomial greatest common divisors turn suitable such elements into factors <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Berlekamp67](#ref-Berlekamp67), [Berlekamp70](#ref-Berlekamp70)]. Randomized algorithms, including Cantor–Zassenhaus <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[CantorZassenhaus81](#ref-CantorZassenhaus81)], obtain expected polynomial running time by finding separating elements with random choices. The difficulty studied here is to make every such choice deterministically, with a bound polynomial in both the degree and the bit length of the characteristic.

Several deterministic results delineate this difficulty. Shoup’s algorithm has polynomial dependence on the degree and a $p^{1/2+o(1)}$ dependence on the characteristic <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Shoup90](#ref-Shoup90)]. Under the generalized Riemann hypothesis, Rónyai obtained polynomial bounds when the number of irreducible factors is bounded <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Ronyai88](#ref-Ronyai88)], and Evdokimov obtained a bound $(n^{\log n}\log p)^{O(1)}$ for general degree <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Evdokimov94](#ref-Evdokimov94)]. The latter is quasipolynomial in $n$. The reduction proved here, combined with the fixed-width zero-free strip that follows immediately from Hecke GRH, already gives a polynomial bound in both parameters under GRH. For Theorem <a href="#thm:factorization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:factorization">1</a>, the companion supplies that strip without GRH. In particular, the hypotheses and parameters of a deterministic result matter: removing random choices alone does not establish a uniform polynomial bound in $(n+1)L$.

Geometric methods already have an important place in this history. Schoof’s elliptic-curve method computes a square root of a fixed integer $a$ modulo a varying prime, when one exists, in time polynomial in $\log p$; the uniform bound in the two parameters is polynomial in $|a|$ and $\log p$, rather than in their binary lengths <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Schoof85](#ref-Schoof85), Section 4]. Pila’s computation of Frobenius on abelian varieties yields deterministic construction of roots of unity of fixed odd prime order modulo primes congruent to $1$ modulo that order, with a running-time exponent that may depend on the order and on the geometric presentation <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Pila90](#ref-Pila90), Theorems A and D]. These results motivate careful accounting for the varying curve and prime in the present construction. We must bound the degrees of field extensions, the sizes of divisor representations, and the dimensions of all linear systems by fixed powers of the original input parameters.

For a single prime not dividing the discriminant of an integral lift, Rónyai obtained, under GRH, deterministic bounds involving the degree of that lift’s splitting field <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Ronyai92](#ref-Ronyai92)]. A different kind of uniformity arises when reductions modulo many primes are processed together. Altman’s unconditional algorithm takes a monic irreducible polynomial in $\mathbb Z[x]$ of coefficient height at most $H\ge2$, with splitting-field degree $m$, and factors its reductions modulo every prime below $X$ in total time $$\pi(X)(m\log H)^{O(1)}\log^5X.$$ This is an amortized result over primes <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Altman2025](#ref-Altman2025), Theorem 1.1]. The parameter $m$ can be as large as the factorial of the polynomial’s degree. Thus this result does not give the worst-case single-input bound sought here. Altman’s introduction also provides a recent account of the general deterministic problem and its remaining obstacles.

The algebraic techniques used below have substantial precedents. Computation at unknown roots by working in a quotient algebra and splitting it when a zero test distinguishes components is an instance of dynamic evaluation <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[DellaDoraDicrescenzoDuval85](#ref-DellaDoraDicrescenzoDuval85), [Duval94](#ref-Duval94)]. The digit calculations in primary multiplicative groups are the prime-power part of the Pohlig–Hellman algorithm <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[PohligHellman78](#ref-PohligHellman78)]. Rónyai already proved that a completely split polynomial of even degree can be split in polynomial time when a quadratic nonresidue is supplied <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Ronyai88](#ref-Ronyai88)]; we include an explicit tournament proof suited to our representation. The odd-degree construction uses cyclic curves $Y^q=F(X)$. The cyclotomic action on their Jacobians and descriptions of its first torsion layer by ramification divisors are familiar from explicit descent, notably the work of Poonen and Schaefer <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[PoonenSchaefer97](#ref-PoonenSchaefer97), Sections 4 and 6].

Our effective treatment of divisors also builds on established computational geometry. Ideal representations on superelliptic curves, Riemann–Roch algorithms, and finite-dimensional linear algebra for Jacobian arithmetic appear in <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[GPS02](#ref-GPS02), [Hess02](#ref-Hess02), [KhuriMakdisi04](#ref-KhuriMakdisi04)]. For norm equations, we use the relation between cyclic algebras and norms, and the approach through maximal orders and minimal left ideals developed in explicit matrix-algebra computations <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[GilleSzamuely06](#ref-GilleSzamuely06), [IRS12](#ref-IRS12), [IKR18](#ref-IKR18)]. The general function-field algorithm of Ivanyos, Kutas, and Rónyai uses a finite-field polynomial-factorization oracle. Here the cyclic presentation gives explicit local orders, and evaluation of their global sections at a rational point gives a rank-one idempotent by linear algebra. The structural explanation uses the splitting of vector bundles on the projective line <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Grothendieck57](#ref-Grothendieck57), [HazewinkelMartin82](#ref-HazewinkelMartin82)].

The substantive step of the present reduction is the odd-degree separator, together with a uniform implementation of the divisor-class divisions it requires. A filtration of lifted ramification classes keeps the necessary field extensions of polynomial degree. A lattice of divisors at infinity then forces two ramification points to receive different labels. We prove this mechanism and its algorithms directly; the earlier results just discussed provide their mathematical context. The separate step of finding sufficiently small auxiliary primes is isolated in Section <a href="#sec:analytic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:analytic">11</a>, which is the only place the companion’s analytic theorem enters.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Proof strategy

The Berlekamp algebra reduces complete factorization to splitting a monic square-free polynomial $F$ all of whose roots already belong to $\mathbf F_p$. Write $N=\deg F\ge2$. The unknown roots are represented simultaneously by the finite algebra $\mathbf F_p[T]/(F)$; an operation whose outcomes differ between components immediately produces a factor by a greatest common divisor. We can therefore describe computations at an unspecified root and carry them out without first finding that root.

The auxiliary primes construct the multiplicative information needed in these computations. For each odd prime $q$, they give a field $K_q=\mathbf F_p(\zeta_q)$ and a generator of the full $q$-primary subgroup of $K_q^*$. The entry for $q=2$ is a generator of the full $2$-primary subgroup of $\mathbf F_p^*$. Here the $q$-primary subgroup consists of the elements whose orders are powers of $q$. These entries permit extraction of promised $q$th roots in finite algebras by small-prime discrete logarithms. Their construction proceeds in increasing $q$: the only factorization needed to make the entry for $q$ has degree $q-1$.

When $N$ is even, differences between pairs of roots and the $2$-primary entry orient the complete graph on the roots. Opposite differences have opposite orientations. The vertex scores cannot all be equal when $N$ is even, so they provide a separator. The more substantial construction handles odd $N$.

Choose an odd prime $q\mid N$, and write $r=v_q(N)$. Over an explicitly constructed field $k$ of degree at most $N(q-1)$ over $\mathbf F_p$, consider the smooth projective curve with affine equation $$C:\quad Y^q=F(X).$$ Its ramification points are $P_i=(x_i,0)$, where the $x_i$ are the unknown roots of $F$. There are $q$ rational points at infinity. The automorphism $\sigma(X,Y)=(X,\zeta_qY)$ acts on degree-zero divisor classes; write $\lambda=1-\sigma$. Degree-zero divisors supported at infinity form a lattice on which $\lambda$ is injective. In contrast, $\lambda$ is surjective on the geometric Jacobian.

This difference is the source of the splitting argument. We lift each class $[P_i-\infty_0]$ repeatedly through $\lambda$, keeping track of an infinity divisor at every stage. A filtration argument shows that all the classes required for these lifts are rational over the field $k$; the extension degree stays polynomial even though the number of possible classes is large. Summing the lifted classes and applying explicit label tests forces a distinction between ramification points. If every test gave one common label, a fixed nonzero infinity divisor would become divisible by one more power of $\lambda$ than its lattice valuation allows.

The geometric existence argument is accompanied by a constructive division algorithm. A promised $\lambda$-division reduces to a norm equation in $k(C)/k(X)$. We solve that equation by constructing maximal orders in a split cyclic algebra and computing their global sections on the projective line. A rank-one idempotent in this algebra yields the norm solution. All computations use polynomial and matrix arithmetic; Riemann–Roch reduction controls divisor sizes, and arithmetic circuits store principal functions arising from large infinity multiplicities. Figure <a href="#fig:route" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:route">1</a> records the roles of these ingredients.

<figcaption>The proof separates auxiliary-prime existence from the algebraic reduction. The dashed implication uses the cited analytic theorem; the remaining constructions are proved in the paper. The two middle branches are the effective and geometric parts of the odd-degree splitter.</figcaption>
</figure>

The technical results are uniform in the degree of the curve and the prime $q$. This uniformity is essential: a running-time exponent depending on the genus would not suffice for Theorem <a href="#thm:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:reduction">2</a>. We give the finite-field tools first, then the auxiliary table, the geometry, and the effective divisor and norm calculations. The splitter and the factorization driver assemble these ingredients. The final two sections prove the bit bounds and the analytic implication.

<a id="ff:section"></a>
### Finite-field computations without known roots

The splitting procedures will manipulate all roots of a polynomial at once. Two elementary tools make this possible. First, a computation over a field can be executed in a product of fields until a zero test distinguishes two components. Second, a supplied generator of a primary multiplicative subgroup permits logarithms and root extraction by short digit calculations. We develop both tools explicitly, and then use them to split every totally split polynomial of even degree.

Finite fields are represented by a basis over their prime field and a multiplication table, or by an equivalent tower of polynomial quotients.
